[RETWEET] Thu Nov 12 18:21:08 +0000 2026 | id 2120945001022841341 | likes 0 | reposts 1 | replies 0
Link: https://x.com/Suprva02/status/2120945001022841341

Every day, cold outreach fills inboxes with the same request.

"Can I get just 15 minutes of your time?"

Sounds harmless, right?

But imagine getting 10 cold pitches a day.

That's 2.5 hours of meetings.

20 pitches? 5 HOURS. Every single day. 💀

And here's the tricky part.

Some pitches might lead to genuinely valuable opportunities.

Others are just wasting time.

You can't reject everyone. You might miss something important.

But you can't accept everyone either. There's only so much room on a calendar.

So what if cold outreach had a TOLL GATE? 😂

Not for your clients.

For people trying to sell YOU something.

Here's how it could work:

Someone wants to pitch you? They submit their pitch and pay a small toll.

If you're interested, they get a meeting.

If you're not, they get their money back.

A way to filter serious pitches without shutting out potentially valuable opportunities.

I recently came across an interesting prediction by @Suprva02 about the future of cold outreach.

And it made me look at CroozFlow from a different angle.

The interesting part? CroozFlow already has the building blocks to make this possible.

I just hadn't thought of using them this way.

Imagine receiving a cold email.

Instead of endless back and forth, the sender gets an automatic reply with your Pitch Mode CroozFlow.

They submit their pitch, pay the toll, and wait for your decision.

Your calendar opens only if you're interested.

No new product needed. Just a different way to use what already exists.

🎬 Meet Pitch Mode CroozFlow.

I'll be building and demonstrating this workflow next Sunday!

Would you charge someone to pitch you, or keep your calendar open to cold outreach? 👀

CroozLink. Your Work-With-Me Link.
@trycroozlink

#BuildInPublic #ColdOutreach #CroozFlow #CroozLink

Here’s what you can do instead of sending cold emails: 

> handwritten letters as image 
> send a spotify playlist named after their problem
> send them a reddit thread where people are asking for exactly what they sell
> personalised voice notes 
> loom videos 
> send them “my mom will get mad if you don’t reply” and other funny DMs referencing a third party 
> comment as outreach 
> memes as outreach 
> send a custom pitch decks
> send gift cards (works)
> tag them in a post where you break down their playbook
> build a tiny tool for their niche and share it free
> show up at their conference 
> cold call them keep it non cringe

outreach is not just cold emails you just need to know what makes them click..

[TWEET] Mon Nov 09 10:30:14 +0000 2026 | id 2119739333846708698 | likes 21 | reposts 1 | replies 9
Link: https://x.com/Suprva02/status/2119739333846708698

Here are the rules to write good cold emails 👇🏻👇🏻👇🏻 (we send cold emails for top YC companies here’s what works)

> send your first 100 cold emails yourself with no help of AI. DO THE WORK YOURSELF 

> write the email for the person who will forward it. "not sure if this is you or someone on your team" gets more replies than the pitch itself

> follow up till you get a direct "no" as a reply

> send more emails to founders than VPs/any other team member 

> tell them why they'll say no before they think it. "you probably already have xyz" kills the objection in line two

> make them feel they're reading about an issue they had no idea they have when they open your cold email

> write the email on your phone to see the length 

> reply to every reply within 2 hours, even the rude ones

> a 1% reply rate on 1000 emails beats a 10% reply rate on 50 the math only works at scale

> 60% of your replies will be "not now". tag them, come back in 90 days that list becomes your best campaign

> stop selling the product sell the cost of the problem staying unsolved for another quarter

> never ask for a call in the first email.

Send this to someone that can help!

[TWEET] Sun Nov 08 09:50:02 +0000 2026 | id 2119366827411456457 | likes 36 | reposts 2 | replies 1

How to be a cracked GTM engineer: 

> think in experiments and first principles 

what worked at company A (similar to you) in 2019 here add ur own stack and approach and experiment you will be surprised how it works…

example: events were a huge thing back then and they are again making a comeback in 2026 

> reverse-engineer competitors: 

find where their customers come from, what converts, what they're missing and how to exploit those gaps.

build ur headlines of outbound campaign and ads based on this

> engineer demand before building distribution: 

find where the money is already moving then find gaps of money getting wasted enter ur product there 

create issues that don’t exist based on the pain points that exist.

build ur distribution around that

[TWEET] Sat Nov 07 12:20:51 +0000 2026 | id 2119042396327719261 | likes 23 | reposts 1 | replies 4
Link: https://x.com/Suprva02/status/2119042396327719261

10 most unethical traits of a great founder 👇🏻👇🏻

> superiority complex: they believe they are better than other people at everything.

> discarding loyal people: if somebody doesn't fit the mission, they're out, even if they were there from day one.

> emotional detachment: they don't feel anything for people. they make the hard call and sleep fine the next day.

> ruthless prioritisation: work comes first. friends and family come second.

> transactional mindset: every relationship has a purpose. if it doesn't, it's out.

> reality distortion: they make people believe in a future that doesn't exist yet.

> over-promising in negotiations: elon musk sold the tesla roadster for 110,000 dollars before he knew if he could build it.

> ignoring the rules: if it doesn't send you to jail, you can do it.

> stealing other people's good ideas: if it's a good idea, it's your idea.

> unreasonable expectations on everyone around them: expecting everyone to sacrifice everything for the mission.

Here’s what you can do instead of sending cold emails: 

> handwritten letters as image 
> send a spotify playlist named after their problem
> send them a reddit thread where people are asking for exactly what they sell
> personalised voice notes 
> loom videos 
> send them “my mom will get mad if you don’t reply” and other funny DMs referencing a third party 
> comment as outreach 
> memes as outreach 
> send a custom pitch decks
> send gift cards (works)
> tag them in a post where you break down their playbook
> build a tiny tool for their niche and share it free
> show up at their conference 
> cold call them keep it non cringe

outreach is not just cold emails you just need to know what makes them click..

We got 12 positive replies from just 30 cold emails.

That's a 40% positive reply rate.

And guess where we found these leads?

OUR SPAM FOLDER 🤭

People kept sending us cold emails that landed in spam.

So we decided to sell to the people trying to sell to us.

> Scraped cold emails from our spam folder.
> Used Prospeo to enrich the companies and filter for our ICP.
> Vibecoded a free cold email analyser using manus ai trained on 1,000+ of our best-performing cold emails.
> Sent it to those prospects as a free lead magnet.

> 30 emails sent.
> 12 positive replies.
> 40% positive reply rate.

We turned our spam folder into a lead list and a vibecoded tool into a lead magnet.

[TWEET] Fri Oct 16 07:37:09 +0000 2026 | id 2110998465434304955 | likes 70 | reposts 3 | replies 20
Link: https://x.com/Suprva02/status/2110998465434304955

2 months ago, I hired someone to make decisions for my life.

Sounds performative. I know.

But I LOVE it.

Here's what they handle for me:

1. What I eat daily. They plan my meals, I approve, my cook does the rest.

2. What to work on first when I have 20 things on my plate and I’m overwhelmed.

3. Travel planning and what places to stay. I hate doing it.

4. A strict daily routine I follow and report back on.

5. A morning check-in. Every day I send a selfie holding a glass of water. If I don’t I lose $100 I’ve made a $1000 deposit to hold me accountable. 

6. My weekly schedule, built around whatever goal I'm chasing that month.

7. Everything that doesn't need my brain. Booking tickets, house hunting, follow-ups.

Small things, right?

But I procrastinate A LOT when left alone.

Trust me following this much strict routine gave 3-4 mental breakdowns in initial stage but once you get through day 8 it becomes easy 

I tried getting friends and my team to keep me on track. Didn't work lol.

As entrepreneur you should absolutely pull this move… You need a system that makes slacking off harder than doing the work.

Give it a try if u have adhd or u procrastinate a lot

[RETWEET] Tue Oct 13 04:06:00 +0000 2026 | id 2109858164804030503 | likes 0 | reposts 3 | replies 0
Link: https://x.com/Suprva02/status/2109858164804030503

Here’s how u can sell to rich people:
3 mins read time 👇🏻

1. use the poor as free ADs without actually spending money

example: 

> canva is free for millions of people making instagram posts.
> those people make canva famous.
> then canva sells team and enterprise plans to companies.

the free crowd isn't the customer. 
they're the ad.

notion does the same so is chatgpt or any other model you see

this is where content comes in play where you speak to the mass while targeting your target audience as well

2. price on what it costs them to not have what you offer

never price on:
> your hours 
> your features

in the first call:

> ask what a lost deal costs them.
> ask what a one month delay costs them.
> ask what a senior engineer's week costs them.

have an roi and a result with a guarantee attached to it 

3. add an unexplainable expensive secondary offer 

> one crazy price makes every other price feel safe.

plan a: $7999, plan b: $100,000 

> movie theatres do the same and even the fancy restaurants u eat at

4. the rich will pay for speed and less risk use that to ur advantage 

> for a big company, every week of waiting costs more than your invoice.

> have a time frame that is faster in your industry and with competitors 

> superhuman charged $30 a month for email when gmail was free.

5. tell them what to do don’t let them decide 

> the poor want options. 
> the rich want answers.

don't send 3 packages and ask which one.

send one. "this is what you need”

rich people pay for not having to think.

the poor make you popular.

the rich make you profitable.

build for both.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

[TWEET] Mon Oct 12 17:11:10 +0000 2026 | id 2109693369052225617 | likes 47 | reposts 3 | replies 6

Here’s how u can sell to rich people:
3 mins read time 👇🏻

1. use the poor as free ADs without actually spending money

example: 

> canva is free for millions of people making instagram posts.
> those people make canva famous.
> then canva sells team and enterprise plans to companies.

the free crowd isn't the customer. 
they're the ad.

notion does the same so is chatgpt or any other model you see

this is where content comes in play where you speak to the mass while targeting your target audience as well

2. price on what it costs them to not have what you offer

never price on:
> your hours 
> your features

in the first call:

> ask what a lost deal costs them.
> ask what a one month delay costs them.
> ask what a senior engineer's week costs them.

have an roi and a result with a guarantee attached to it 

3. add an unexplainable expensive secondary offer 

> one crazy price makes every other price feel safe.

plan a: $7999, plan b: $100,000 

> movie theatres do the same and even the fancy restaurants u eat at

4. the rich will pay for speed and less risk use that to ur advantage 

> for a big company, every week of waiting costs more than your invoice.

> have a time frame that is faster in your industry and with competitors 

> superhuman charged $30 a month for email when gmail was free.

5. tell them what to do don’t let them decide 

> the poor want options. 
> the rich want answers.

don't send 3 packages and ask which one.

send one. "this is what you need”

rich people pay for not having to think.

the poor make you popular.

the rich make you profitable.

build for both.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

here’s how to avoid situations like this 👇🏻

Before sending any message:

   → DO NOT SEND

> learn sales or find a business partner who is in sales
> market CRAZY

If market fit is there you can easily get to to 2-5 lakhs/mo or even more within months

But don’t get into it by thinking only you will make money. Because if money is the only goal you will make 0. 

Better get a decent job.

If you see a pain point you can solve and wanna challenge your skill set go for it.

[TWEET] Mon Oct 05 13:26:38 +0000 2026 | id 2107100148731920384 | likes 44 | reposts 2 | replies 6
Link: https://x.com/Suprva02/status/2107100148731920384

Will outbound work for you or content or partnerships? Here’s how you can figure out 👇🏻 (long post ahead)

Welcome to Day 1 of learning GTM.

Today I’ll show you the main GTM models and when to use them.

1. Pick your growth motion:

Choose based on deal size + buyer behaviour.

→ Under $5K ACV: product-led / marketing-led
→ $5K to $50K: sales-led + content
→ $50K + enterprise sales + ABM

Most companies use 2 motions together.

2. Win one niche first

Don’t sell to everyone from day one.

Start with one narrow market, get customers + proof, then move into the next similar market.

Example:

Series A SaaS companies with 20–50 sales reps

3. Create a category

Don’t only say:

“We’re faster.”
“We’re cheaper.”
“We have more features.”

Make buyers see the problem differently.

Name the problem → teach the market → become known for solving it.

4. Use existing ecosystems

Your best prospects may already buy from companies around you.

Find where your customers overlap with theirs.

Then target that overlap first.

5. Use warm paths before cold outreach
Before emailing a stranger, ask:

Who already knows this buyer?

Customer
Partner
Investor
Ex-colleague
Community member

You → trusted connection → buyer
usually beats
You → stranger

6. Let product usage trigger sales
Don’t call every free signup.

Call when buying signals appear:

→ multiple users join
→ usage increases
→ limits are hit
→ manager gets invited
Usage tells you when sales should step in.

7. Start small, then expand

Don’t always chase the biggest contract first.

Start with one team.

Prove value.

Then expand:

20 users → 50 users → another team → company-wide

Tomorrow we will learn how to pick the right signals

Send this post to your team!

[TWEET] Sat Oct 03 10:42:10 +0000 2026 | id 2106333983864742151 | likes 24 | reposts 2 | replies 2
Link: https://x.com/Suprva02/status/2106333983864742151

Idk who needs to hear this but being slow costs more money than taking actions. 

I was slow AF here are tiny changes I did in my routine to get the shit done and make more money:

> trained my mind first. every gym session and reps, i repeated the words i wanted done that week in present tense while doing the reps.

> wrote my to do list before sleeping without missing a single day (yes even weekends)

> made health a priority. a lot of my slowness was just low vitamins.

> took one small action the moment i thought of something new, then told my team so they would push me.

> set alarms for priority tasks on my phone and laptop.

> visualised a lot. what my life looks like if i do the thing. it kept me focused and obsessed.

> did only 1 task at a time. i used to juggle 3-5 and wonder why i was so tired. no more multitasking.

> used mel robbins' 5 second rule. count 5-4-3-2-1 and move everytime mind asks me to rest or overthink.

> forced myself. i spent a lot of money on things so i had no choice but to do them.

> every time my mind avoided a task, i pictured myself doing it.

> did one thing every single day, no matter what.

example: if i want to grow on X, i post at least once a day. viral or not, i don't care.

i know this sounds like a lot… but if you want something you need to go to the extremes to get it.

[TWEET] Wed Sep 30 09:27:51 +0000 2026 | id 2105228120144093668 | likes 32 | reposts 3 | replies 1
Link: https://x.com/Suprva02/status/2105228120144093668

hey if u book 0 sales calls it’s mostly because u sell to the guys who don’t need wtv u selling (use this formula to book more sales calls) 

do this today to fix: 

1. check the market: 

open apollo → add ur filter (of companies) you target→ see how many buyers exist

then who u sell to:

if you sell to marketing companies add that and see how many of them are there

then add what u sell:

add ur own industry nxt and see how many of you are there?

2. compare: 

let’s say: 

buyers: 5,000
competitors: 500

use this formula to calculate saturation

> competitors ÷ buyers × 100 = saturation %

like: 

> 500 competitors ÷ 5,000 buyers × 100 = 10%**

the higher this % → the more saturated the market.

4. compare demand vs supply

lots of buyers + weak supply = opportunity

5. gap analysis 

add what is missing and launch a small outreach campaign (5000 emails min) to judge the situation. 

if you get 2% more reply rate u know offer is good. 

then add workflows tools to it and scale.

To actually get paid customers from cold emailing you need to have these skills: (no BS edition) 👇🏻

> proper infra set-up: if ur emails lands in spam nothing else matters

> niche offer: if the offer isn’t good they won’t reply

> good case study: this helps u land high ticket deals from emails

if you don’t have all the above skills cold email won’t work for you.

[TWEET] Thu Sep 24 05:33:49 +0000 2026 | id 2102994897330753805 | likes 41 | reposts 1 | replies 17

when i used to take 5-7 calls every single day while being stuck at agency growth i questioned everything in life

which made me realise more calls isn’t growth at all 

did some changes:

> changed positioning and focused on less clients but higher ticket 
> removed retainers added project and result based deals 
> only started working with clients that have an existing outbound team/system 
> stopped focusing on inbound leads 
> doubled down on outbound and referral systems 
> started tracking proper metrics in terms of roi 
> hired agency partners to handle the backend 

I used to obsess over taking more and more sales calls it made me feel that i am doing great while ignoring the quality of work and getting burned out. 

i had a good close rate but still most clients would drop out in a month or 3 month we realised quality is where we are lacking 

i joined a lot of courses and mentorships and got an advice from a guy doing over $20M in arr he told me to not focus on calls focus on quality 

we implemented the things he mentioned and profit went through roof while living a better life

the changes we did in the last 6 months helped us build a very good base and get some of the top SaaS companies in this space 

still not perfect on what im doing but it’s better than what it was last year

stop asking discovery questions on sales calls if you book them through outbound 

we booked 32 something calls for a SaaS company they closed 0 

(story time) 

our process is pretty simple:

> we run the outbound
> get positive replies
> then give the sales team all the in-depth research we have on each prospect
> we ask them to look at the data and make a roadmap to show their prospects on the call

but instead of using it…

they followed traditional product demos asking a lot of questions and giving no value 

noone seriously care about ur saas features so stop showing features 

show them a picture of how their day to day life would look like using ur product and show them how much research u have done 

same thing with service based business or any kind of business  

u are reaching out to them have a good point on call on why you reached out to them

people building AI don’t use AI google engineer told me they don’t give a f*** about ai💀

while travelling to blr I had a conversation with a guy who handles tech at google (prev tesla and amazon)

i asked him what all ai tools they use? 

he said “none”

his line: "one bad paste and our source code lives on someone else's server."

and it's not just google.

> samsung banned chatgpt in 2023 after engineers pasted chip source code into it.

> apple locked out chatgpt and copilot while quietly building its own.

> jpmorgan, goldman, verizon. blocked at the firewall.

the biggest tech companies on earth run on internal tools their own teams built years ago.

boomer stacks… on purpose.

his reasons, the ones that actually matter:

1. your prompts are training data. most ai tools reserve the right to learn from what you type. 

their roadmap inside a competitor's model is not a risk, it's a gift.

2. license contamination. copilot got sued in 2022 for spitting out copyrighted code. 

one gpl snippet in a closed product and legal has to open source the repo. a billion dollar mistake from a single autocomplete.

3.cost at scale. per token pricing works for 20 people. google runs billions of internal calls a day. 

cheaper to own the chips. amazon built trainium for this. tesla built dojo.

every big company that refuses to use ai is a company that has to build ai.

so if you're building an ai product on X right now, one question decides whether you sell to a 20 person startup or a 20,000 person enterprise:

does the data leave the building?

if yes, you're competing with every founder on this app.

if no, you're competing with almost nobody.

you have no legal department and nothing that ends up in a headline if it leaks. 

you get to move fast because you have nothing to protect yet.

save this for the next time someone says you're behind.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

the peer mirror only works if the peers are similar.

don't compare a local cafe to starbucks.

don't compare a 20-person startup to nike.

same market. same size. same stage.

that's what makes them feel behind.

I get a lot of requests from SaaS founders asking me to help them with GTM in exchange for equity, rev share, or a co-founder offer.

I reject the majority of them. Here's why:

Most of these founders are chasing the status of raising from YC or a16z before figuring out if they have a product people will even pay for..

Then they expect just marketing will fix everything.

Look at cluely once distribution alone wont do anything if product sucks
 
We work with some of the largest YC companies, and the reality of raising funds is way different than what you see online..

I see products raising millions fail every week..

That’s why I say… try bootstrapping ur saas to $30K MRR before raising funds (unless market demands)

It forces you to learn what actually matters: customers, retention, distribution, and revenue funnels 

And more over u will know if this is really the SaaS you wanna build and lock in for

I have seen saas founders get depressed after raising funds because they realise no-one wants their product and now they have pressure from investors to scale fast :(((

But I would like to help you if you've got an existing MVP, with a real moat, feel free to DM me.

I'll help you figure out your GTM.

[TWEET] Tue Sep 15 09:41:21 +0000 2026 | id 2099795699810402594 | likes 54 | reposts 1 | replies 28
Link: https://x.com/Suprva02/status/2099795699810402594

Someone just lost $30K worth of SaaS deals by building in public.

I just got off a call with a SaaS founder who hired a LinkedIn agency.

They interviewed him for 2 hours.

Then started posting EVERYTHING.

> Sales calls.
> Customer stories.
> What customers were asking for.
> His strategy.
> Even details about enterprise customers.

Then one of those enterprise customers saw a post.

They pulled out of the deal.

They didn’t want anyone knowing they were evaluating the product.

And it got worse.

His “success story” content started attracting a ton of new SaaS founders asking for advice.

He was getting attention from the wrong people while making his existing prospects uncomfortable.

The positioning was completely wrong.

This is the biggest mistake I see SaaS founders make with LinkedIn or X

They think building in public means sharing everything.

It doesn’t.

You can be transparent without exposing your entire playbook.

We’ve generated 50,000+ leads for SaaS companies through LinkedIn, and this is how I’d approach founder-led content:

→ post wins, but connect the win to your product.

Hit $100K MRR? Don’t just post the screenshot. Tell the story of how you used your own product to get there.

@fin465 origami’s founder does this well

→ show end results, not implementation.

Teach people how to think and key rules Don’t hand competitors your entire system.

→ stop posting customer information without consent.

Your customer’s privacy > your content calendar.

→ create more “how-to” content.

Make your ICP save your posts because they actually solve problems.

→ get your team involved.

Multiple people creating useful content + lead magnets gives you multiple distribution channels.

→ build a funnel behind the content.

Likes are not leads. Use soft CTAs, lead magnets and warm follow-up to turn attention into pipeline.

→ post images of the work.

Screenshots. Product demos. Team photos. Behind-the-scenes. Real stuff > polished corporate content.

→ build public led challenges around your product. (Replit does this) 

30-day challenges, live builds, webinars, experiments. Give people a reason to participate.

→ talk about buying triggers.

What happens right before someone needs your product?

A new VP gets hired.

The team doubles.

And here’s the simple content formula:

1 problem
↓
How you solved it
↓
Your product’s role in the solution
↓
A story/proof point
↓
Insight > implementation

Your content should make your ICP think:

“Holy shit, that’s exactly what we’re dealing with.”

Not:

“Wow, this founder is posting relatable content”

Build in public if you want.

Just remember:

The internet doesn’t need to know everything you’re building.

Especially in SaaS.

Your competitors are watching too.

[TWEET] Mon Sep 14 18:01:28 +0000 2026 | id 2099559170735554715 | likes 41 | reposts 1 | replies 13
Link: https://x.com/Suprva02/status/2099559170735554715

If Anthropic gives me a job, I might quit my agency.

And no, it’s not because of the “money” or life security 

This might sound stupid to a lot of founders.

But I actually think working at a great company could make me a much better founder.

I have never had a real job.

I started freelancing when I was very young and eventually built my own agency.

So almost everything I know about business, I learned by figuring shit out myself.

But there’s one thing that changed my perspective.

An internship I did in 2022 at a startup for a year… it was a data analytics startup they used to build AI ML models everything was still very new about AI back then but I learned so much of things 

I learned more in a few months than I had learned in years of trying to figure things out alone.

And it made me realize something:

There is a massive difference between learning something yourself and learning it from people who are already world class at it.

When you run an agency, you spend a lot of time figuring things out.

> Hiring.
> Selling.
> Managing clients.
> Building systems.
> Fixing problems.
> Making mistakes.

But you also have no idea what you’re missing.

> You don’t know what a great product team looks like if you’ve never been around one.

> You don’t know how exceptional people make decisions if you’ve never worked with them.

> You don’t know what “operating at scale” actually looks like until you’ve seen it.

And I think that’s where a high-value job can be insanely valuable.

You can compress years of learning into months.

You get to sit around people who are ridiculously good at what they do.

You see how they think.

But yeah being at a job won’t be a permanent thing till I learn whatever I wanted

[TWEET] Fri Sep 11 14:22:03 +0000 2026 | id 2098416788702965762 | likes 8 | reposts 0 | replies 2
Link: https://x.com/Suprva02/status/2098416788702965762

SOPs on notion SUCKSSS

Do this instead:

> add tella’s mcp to claude 
> create videos of manual work (once)
> add this prompt to claude: 👇🏻

“Watch the Tella videos I’ve provided and learn how I do the task. Understand the workflow, tools, inputs, decisions, and desired output from the recordings. Don’t just document what I did. Figure out how to reproduce the result yourself.
If something is unclear, ask me instead of guessing. Once you understand the workflow, execute it yourself whenever I give you the same task.”

> build skills of each repeated work 
> ask ur team to do manual work once and compare it to the skills claude built
> recreate things that dont make sense
> explain your team core principles 
> experiment with 5 logic at once
> store the one that works 

this is the best way to get things done without explaining everything again or building SOPs.

If you are new to cold email do this (mini course) 

> create 10 gmail accounts 
> warm them up for 20 days
> use apollo’s free extension on LinkedIn 
> get their emails for free 
> verify using name chip on gsheet (free) 
> start emailing (15/email id/day) 
> don’t skip weekends be mad and obsessed 
> make the emails super creative 
> handwritten letters, memes, funny PPTs 
> set aside 5 hrs every single day to just sending cold emails manually 
> warm up each email once in 2 weeks
> after 3 weeks if still 0 reply your offer sucks
> a/b test with 5-6 offers (dw u will figure out)
> follow up till they block you 
> dont take their no as an answer 
> show up at their office with sweet 
> have a niche offer 
> target industries where competition is low
> aim for 300+ emails a week
> write emails they can scan not read
> target the top 1% of a niche industry 
> write all cold emails manually 
> use claude for research 
> use reddit for validation of offer 
> ask 50 people on Reddit every week
> add LinkedIn as well in ur outreach 

Watch how you sign them as clients 

Seriously people over complicate cold emails

👉 No you don’t need:

- 100 tools 
- clay subscription 
- to spend $1000+ 
- to be a tech expert 
- to take a course 

Cold emails are fking simple 

If anything you need to learn is copywriting. 

That you can learn by sending cold emails and getting rejected. 

Nothing teaches you better than live experience know that.

[TWEET] Wed Sep 09 05:37:02 +0000 2026 | id 2097559887601672521 | likes 27 | reposts 1 | replies 3
Link: https://x.com/Suprva02/status/2097559887601672521

Pay attention to this post if you use Claude, Grok, or ChatGPT seriously. 👇🏻

Please please pleaseeee i beg you 

use this prompt:

“Before you start [TASK], I want you to become an expert at it first.

Spend the first part of the job researching how the best people are actually doing this today.

Look across YouTube, Substack, Reddit, GitHub, blogs, X, podcasts, case studies, and other high-quality sources. Find roughly 15–30 of the strongest and most relevant examples from the last 6 months.

Study the actual material, not just the headlines. Pull transcripts, documentation, examples, code, frameworks, or whatever source material is available.

Then compare everything you found.

Look for:

Tactics that multiple experts recommend
Patterns across successful examples
Contradictory opinions
Specific techniques that produce better results

Give more weight to insights that show up repeatedly across independent sources, especially when they’re backed by real examples or results.

Once you’re done, turn the research into one practical playbook for [TASK].

Don’t give me a research summary.

Actually absorb the research, build the skill, and then use it to do the task.”

Before you ask it to do anything important, make it study the best people already doing that thing.

I’ve done this for almost every skill I use.

AI gets dramatically better when you stop making it guess what “good” looks like.

Save this and try it today.

This is the easiest way to close high ticket deals (through referrals)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[TWEET] Tue Sep 08 15:53:16 +0000 2026 | id 2097352578388607230 | likes 20 | reposts 2 | replies 8
Link: https://x.com/Suprva02/status/2097352578388607230

If you suck at cold outreach build a referral system instead 

I learned it from my friend whose business runs on referral game

He books 10–15 sales calls/month with this funnel.

The system looks like this:

1. Build a referral database

> Put every client, ex-client, partner, consultant, investor, founder and agency owner into one table.

> Track who they know, what buyers they know, trust level, referrals sent, calls booked and deals closed.

2. Find the connectors

> Don’t just target buyers.

> Target people who already have access to your buyers like

> Fractional CROs  
> Consultants  
> Investors  
> Recruiters  
> Community operators  
> RevOps freelancers  
> Adjacent agencies

3. Score your connectors

> Score them on access, trust, frequency and relevance.

> Prioritize the people who have the most access to your ideal customers and the most trust with them.

4. Create referral triggers

> Never ask randomly.

> Trigger the ask when a client gets a result, hits a milestone, gives positive feedback, renews or finishes a project successfully.

> Result → trigger → referral ask.

5. Ask specific questions

> Never say “Who do you know?”

> Ask: “Which SaaS founder have you spoken to recently who complained about pipeline?”

> Specific questions produce names.

6. Make referrals effortless

> Give your connector:

> One-line explanation  
> Forwardable message  
> Case study / teardown / audit

> They shouldn’t have to sell you.

> The referral should take 30 seconds.

7. Create a referral asset

> Give them something they can send before asking for the intro.

> A teardown, benchmark, checklist, case study or short Loom.

> Recommendation → proof → conversation.

8. Build the referral pipeline

> Target connector → Trigger → Ask → Name → Intro → Call → Deal

> Now you know exactly where referrals are getting stuck.

9. Follow up

> Day 0 → Ask  
> Day 3 → Specific follow-up  
> Day 7 → Send something useful  

> Don’t chase people forever.

10. Make every sales call create another referral

> They buy → ask for a referral.

> They don’t buy → ask for a referral.

Ask “Who else do you know dealing with this problem?”

One introduction can create two more.

11. Nurture your connectors

> Every month, send them something useful.

> Stay top of mind so you become the person they think of when someone needs what you sell.

12. Track 5 numbers

> Referral asks → Names → Introductions → Calls → Deals

Example:

40 asks → 25 names → 20 intros → 15 calls → 6 deals

> Now referrals are a measurable acquisition channel.

13. Set a weekly quota

> 20 referral asks  
> 10 connector conversations  
> 5 referral assets sent  
> 5 introductions requested  
> 3 old connectors reactivated

Now 10–15 calls/month becomes a target you can work toward instead of something you hope happens.

14. Double down on your best connectors

> Track who sends the most qualified introductions.

> If one consultant sends you 8 great leads and another sends zero, don’t treat them equally.

> Spend more time with the people already producing.

That’s how you turn referrals from random favors into a predictable acquisition channel.

Save this for later!

One great thing about time is that… 
it changes.

Sounds simple, right?

> The problem you have today might not exist a few months from now.

> The thing you are bad at today might become your strength later.

We judge ourselves based on our current time. 

But time changes… then why does our perception remains the same? 

Don’t judge yourself too soon

Right now is not forever.

SaaS companies are paying $100,000 to launch companies only to be hyped up by bots.

I see a lot of hype around product launches on X.

These launch agencies charge $100k for a single launch.

That doesn’t even include influencer marketing funds

Most launch agencies here run bot farms.

SaaS companies are running behind hype and it’s becoming an ego game rather than marketing stunt.

These agencies guarantee 5M+ views per launch. 

But getting that amount of views is easy when you run an army of bots on X

The goal of a launch should be:

- Build distribution
- Get people aware of your product
- Get more leads

Yet 80% of launch agencies on X are doing shitty work.

It’s my favourite thing because thanks to them, we have a trigger.

Every time we see a launch on X, we reach out to the team and ask:

“Did you get the results you were looking for?”

Most give us negative feedback.

We convert them as clients 

If you want to study great launches..

- look at @WisprFlow launch
- @lemma_ ’s launch for true storytelling - @askOkara launch (they have made a playbook on it)
- GPT’s Astra video launch is insane too.

Study these.

The goal isn’t just views.

The goal is to get people talking about your product.

And that only happens when a bot army isn’t the sole reason behind the virality.

Advice to SaaS founders:

If you don’t have a GTM plan to turn your launch into sales, don’t launch yet hire a GTM partner before you hire a launch agency! 

There are ofc great launch agencies here but what I’m saying is most are not so authentic.

[TWEET] Sun Sep 06 08:58:48 +0000 2026 | id 2096523498801791124 | likes 35 | reposts 1 | replies 31
Link: https://x.com/Suprva02/status/2096523498801791124

I have lost over $100,000 building a business 

Which led me to change almost everything on how I run a business👇🏻

1. The source of your clients matters more than the number of clients.

40% of our clients used to come from outbound.

60% came from inbound.

The problem?

The quality of inbound clients wasn’t good enough.

So we stopped taking inbound clients altogether. (Exceptions are there)

Now:

40% of our clients come from referrals.

60% come from outbound.

The quality is WAY better.

2. Don’t sell cold email to someone who has never done cold email.

I used to target B2B companies that had never tried outbound.

Huge mistake.

They didn’t understand how it worked.

Most couldn’t convert the replies we got them

And most wouldn’t listen when we told them their offer needed to change.

Churn was huge.

3. Don’t bundle everything into one offer.

I was charging $3K/month for:

LinkedIn content + outbound + cold email.

That was everything wrong with my offer.

We were trying to solve too many problems with one retainer.

Now we only work with companies that already have an in-house outbound team.

We come in as an add-on.

We shifted our offer and pricing both 👇🏻

4. Align your pricing with the outcome.

We completely removed retainers.

Now we charge a fixed fee + % of closed revenue.

We work on 6-month projects with a guaranteed offer within the first 2 months.

The incentives are much better for everyone.

5. Hiring is harder than it looks.

We spent way too much time finding and training people.

Even with good salaries, we realised some things simply can’t be taught.

So we stopped trying to make bad fits work.

We fired anyone who wasn’t a fit.

6. Partnerships can beat hiring.

Instead of building everything internally, we started onboarding agencies as partners.

We bring the client.

They do the work.

We rev share every deal.

One of our client deals is now worth $80,000

And even after shifting to agency partnerships, we grew 40% more in less than 3 months.

7. Growth should make your life better, not just your revenue bigger.

Now clients are happier.

We’re working with premium clients.

I have more time on my calendar.

And I’m making more than I was when I was managing everything myself.

I still want to build a great team.

But instead of making bad hires and hoping they work out

I’m investing in courses, coaching and consultation to learn how to build a business.

The biggest lesson:

You don’t always need to add more to grow.

Sometimes you need to remove everything that’s attracting the wrong clients, creating the wrong incentives and making the business harder to run.

What I’m doing next:

1. Build something for our inbound leads

I get 50+ leads a week from inbound alone.

Instead of letting them go to waste, I’m building something that can help them and make sure they still have a great experience with us.

2. Build SaaS + apps

I don’t want to rely only on agency revenue.

I have a few SaaS and app ideas I want to build.

I just need to find a great technical co-founder.

I’ve spent years working with SaaS companies on GTM, so I know how to help scale a company from $0 to $100,000.

Now I want to build some products of my own.

3. Niche down

Right now we work with B2B SaaS companies across tech, AI and marketing.

We help them with GTM.

But I want to go much deeper into one specific industry.

4. Build a real team

I want to build a hybrid team that works both offline and online.

I realised being completely remote isn’t always the solution.

Some things are just better when people are in the same room.

5. Add physical distribution to our outreach funnel

We’re experimenting with physical distribution as part of our outreach strategy.

More on this soon.

[TWEET] Sat Sep 05 19:33:57 +0000 2026 | id 2096320950572888262 | likes 31 | reposts 0 | replies 10
Link: https://x.com/Suprva02/status/2096320950572888262

Content has made me $2 Million + in sales and got me 10,000+ leads.

(probably more I’m not counting everything properly)

Almost all of it started with LinkedIn.

I started creating content in 2023.

At first, I just posted whatever was working for me.

Then started on Instagram again I had no idea I made wtv I liked

Since then, LinkedIn + Instagram have been my main demand engines.

Here’s what I learned:

LinkedIn = #1 for me in business value

60% of our clients came from inbound until 2025.

A lot of them turned into year-long clients.

Instagram = completely different

In 2024, I had a Skool community where I taught lead gen to young agency owners.

I eventually shut it down because the goal was different and it wasn’t making much.

Then in 2025, I started taking brand deals seriously.

Worked with Replit, Manus, Gamma and more.

Eventually, I figured out how to turn some of those brand relationships into high-paying clients.

That made Instagram much more valuable for me. 

Now I’m moving to X + YouTube.

I don’t really have a system for either yet.

But I’ve noticed:

1. LinkedIn = sales focused content works well+ people act like bots

2. Instagram = beginner friendly + lots of “get rich quick” content works well here audience can be shallow if not mindful 

3. X + YouTube = depth seems to work better here i noticed long form content on both channels doing well

I used to think YouTube works on clickbait but everything is way different there that I thought 

So I’m building a system from scratch.

Will be experimenting a lot on both lets see what these two brings me in the long run 

I’ll share what works, what doesn’t, and the results along the way.

[TWEET] Sat Sep 05 11:09:55 +0000 2026 | id 2096194107307966533 | likes 13 | reposts 1 | replies 2
Link: https://x.com/Suprva02/status/2096194107307966533

i learned this sales lesson from a guy doing over $3m/mo

top salespeople keep their offers stupidly simple.

1. delete every deliverable that doesn’t lead to a result

don’t sell 12 posts, 8 videos, 4 calls.

sell the result.

2. one problem → one solution

don’t try to solve 5 different problems with one offer.

pick one painful problem and solve it.

3. outcome + number + time → vague promises

“we help you grow”

↓

“we help you get 10,000 new users in 90 days.”

make the result tangible.

4. 13 year old test → complicated explanation

explain your offer to someone who understands business but has zero context on what you do.

if they don’t understand it immediately, simplify it.

5. one buyer → one clear offer

don’t make your offer for everyone.

make it feel like it was built specifically for the person reading it.

the simpler your offer is to understand,

the easier it is to sell.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

[TWEET] Mon Aug 31 12:42:01 +0000 2026 | id 2094405346450427980 | likes 6 | reposts 0 | replies 3
Link: https://x.com/Suprva02/status/2094405346450427980

How to know if you are landing in the spam folder? (Process 👇🏻)

You can’t directly see “spam placement” inside Smartlead ai but you can catch the warning signs before the whole campaign tanks.

Here’s how:

1. Go to Smartlead → Insights.

2. Check Reply Rate per Mailbox.

If one mailbox suddenly drops while the others are stable, that’s the first one to investigate.

3. Check Bounce Rate.

Insights flags mailboxes trending toward the danger zone before the fleet average hides it.

4. Check Reply Tracking.

It tells you if IMAP, OAuth, permissions, or the mail service itself broke.

5. Check Blocked Mailboxes.

Smartlead tells you whether the inbox needs a reconnect, cooldown, or replacement.
Then hit Summarise.

It gives you a plain-English breakdown of what’s wrong, what was already fixed, and what still needs your approval.

So instead of waiting 2–3 weeks for campaign-level reply rates to collapse, you can catch the exact mailbox causing the problem much earlier.

Let me know if you need a detailed guide on this?

PS: Martin got no chill

[TWEET] Sun Aug 30 17:57:02 +0000 2026 | id 2094122234139062779 | likes 20 | reposts 1 | replies 3

i accidentally found a business model in 2020 that is a $50B industry today (full story👇🏻)

in 2020, i had a burner X account where i shitposted all day

meme pages kept reposting my stuff

90% of it would go viral

my friends would literally send me the same posts + posted on their stories

without knowing it was me

then i noticed something interesting

every time a post blew up

brands started showing up

tech companies

FMCG brands

big names

they would literally pay me to make fun content around their products

i noticed…

they were paying for attention

one viral post could put a brand in front of hundreds of thousands of people

i made around $15K from this

that felt insane tbh at that time

because i was super young 

and then i made the classic mistake

i thought:

“F*ck it, i’ll turn this into a company”

the idea was actually pretty simple

build meme pages around individual brands fully dedicated to them 

create content people actually want to consume

then turn that attention into sales

basically branded media

i kind of did it for 2 months but i was not very professional i couldn’t deliver things at time and wnv a brand didn’t approve my idea i didn’t like it

most brands coming to me had boomer decision makers who had no taste 

so i used to get irritated i also had huge ego back then i couldn’t tolerate “hearing no” to my ideas that i thought was good

i worked with customers that had no sense of humour

i was doing everything on my own because finding creative folks was a task too 

so i gave up on the idea 

and this business died before anything can start 

looking back, i probably stopped way too early

because the opportunity was much bigger than i understood

attention was becoming a product

and today, creators + meme pages + niche media can be distribution channels worth billions 

if you can consistently capture attention from a specific audience

there’s probably a business hiding underneath it

i still think about it

apart from this i also had 10+ ideas sitting in my diary 

just proves ideas alone do nothing

[TWEET] Sun Aug 30 13:18:24 +0000 2026 | id 2094052114771411417 | likes 24 | reposts 2 | replies 7
Link: https://x.com/Suprva02/status/2094052114771411417

0-100 paying customers full GTM without spending too much $$👇🏻

> don’t use freemium strategy that’s how u attract broke unserious people rather have a strong paid offer and a 7 day trial since day 1

> build the polymarket of your own product, publish new data, rankings, benchmarks and predictions every week let people bet on it let them be wrong(more engagement better)

> build complimentary github repos turn them into lead magnets make the data shareable and let your product sit behind it

> a job post is not just a hiring signal, read the description for the problems they are actually hiring someone to solve

> take your best 20 customers and find what they have in common that your original icp never mentioned 

> start with a tiny offer for a very specific audience, not a giant saas with 14 use cases

> find 100 people already paying your competitor and start there, they already have the pain and the budget

> don’t build an affiliate program, find agencies already selling the outcome you provide and let them package your product into their service

> give agencies rev share, but make the product increase their margins or results so they have a reason to actually push it

> find the agencies with 20-100 clients, one good agency partner can be worth more than 100 individual customers

> turn your customers into your influencer network, help them get a result and give them a reason to talk about how they got it

> if people are sharing the insight without being asked, you’ve built a distribution layer

> don’t create content around what you think people want, create content around what your customers are actually doing inside the product

> “we analyzed 10,000 campaigns” will usually beat “5 reasons you should use our saas”

> cold outreach should have 7+ segments before you start adding volume

> don’t segment only by industry and title, segment by the event that makes them likely to buy

> a new cmo, cmo hiring demand gen, cmo launching a product and cmo replacing a competitor are four completely different campaigns

> stack signals instead of relying on one, “saas + cmo” is an icp, “saas + new cmo + hiring demand gen + raised recently” is better

> scrape 1000+ job descriptions a week, extract the repeated problems and turn those problems into your outbound segments

> once one segment works, add volume to it before inventing another strategy

> freemium becomes much more interesting after you have enough paying users to understand activation, retention and your real power users

> build a community around the problem, not around your product

> build complementary github repos that solve small pieces of the problem your saas solves

> reddit works when you enter existing conversations, not when you drop your landing page into 20 subreddits

> use voice dms for high-value prospects

> send handwritten notes to your top accounts

> make personalized memes for prospects where the joke itself demonstrates that you understand their problem

> send a mini ppt when a normal dm would take 500 words to explain

> use loom when seeing the problem is more powerful than reading about it

> creative outreach works because everyone else is sending the same boring message

> don’t do high-effort outreach for everyone, pick your top 50-100 accounts and make them impossible to ignore

> then go find 100 more companies that look like those 20

> once you find a winning offer, build multiple funnels around it instead of constantly launching new offers

[TWEET] Thu Aug 27 05:24:03 +0000 2026 | id 2092845577721778223 | likes 28 | reposts 3 | replies 7
Link: https://x.com/Suprva02/status/2092845577721778223

THIS POST WILL GET A LOT OF HATE BUT IDC:

you don’t need to suffer to build a $200k/mo business. (easiest way read till end)

there’s a popular belief that your first 1-2 years in business are supposed to be painful.

> get cheated by clients  
> get fucked by employees  
> make terrible hires  
> deal with delivery disasters  
> lose money  
> learn everything the hard way

which is true 95% time

but u can at-least try the 5% way here’s how:

there’s a concept called comparative advantage.

find someone who is better at the thing you’re bad at.

meaning…

if you’re great at the actual service 

but u suck at hiring ans building a team as good as you then

WHITE-LABEL YOUR OFFER

atleast till you hit $200k/mo. 

then build the team. (u will learn from the partner how to)

while the business run in backend you do all this:

> build your distribution (this has a lot of advantage in the long run)
> be the face of the offer  
> create demand through content  
> do sales for ur own offer
> give them your sops + training 
> keep 2-3 people in-house to take over when shit goes south  
> split 40/60 or 50/50

why i like this model:

**separate distribution from delivery**  

> you focus on acquisition, positioning and sales while they focus on fulfilment.

sales is what gonna help u in the long run not fulfilment
 
**specialise aggressively**  

> you become world-class at creating demand instead of trying to master every function in the company.

**create leverage**  

> one strong SOP + with training videos can be deployed across dozens of clients through an existing team.

u just gotta teach

**reduce execution risk**  

> your in-house team gives you a backup layer when delivery starts slipping.

if you sell a $5k package: (example)

20 clients = $100k/mo
40 clients = $200k/mo

this way u can scale fast by focusing on volume rather than keeping stuck at building a team 

first do the work that feels easy to you because you’re f*cking good at it.

a lot of procrastination happens because 

you’re just spending your best hours on work that your brain doesn’t like to start.

>you hate it.
>you avoid it.
>you become slower.

eventually it starts affecting your confidence, energy and everything else.

instead:

specialise early.

build the part of the business where you have the most advantage.

and…

STOP romanticising suffering.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

(Reply to @ankit_lachhwani)

follow diff sales call framework for both

1. inbound lead: 

> send them success stories 30 mins before call
> let them talk you listen 
> have 2 call close where the first acts as discovery second as close 
> don’t convince them or pitch anything but check if they deserve ur offer in tje first place 

here you have the most power 

for outbound:

> do not ask sales questions have everything ready
> show them why you reached them
> have images + mindmaps that are relevant to them where you walk them through first 6 weeks
> use this framework: 

1) what you noticed (issue)
2) who else is suffering (similar companies they know of) 
3) why you (mention best case studies + client emails they can ask for review) 
4) why now? show them what already gone and how it can be improved 
5) risk free trial they can opt out anytime

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

[TWEET] Wed Aug 26 06:36:44 +0000 2026 | id 2092501482327412950 | likes 14 | reposts 2 | replies 7

1. Stripe and Airbnb’s founders used to knock on doors getting rejected 

2. Instagram founders used to cold email tech people to join Instagram 

3. Hubspot’s founder did intensive cold calls to get its first users 

4. Reddit founders manually cold emailed a lot of bloggers to create posts on reddit

5. Hormozi used to do cold calling for gym launch every single day

6. Zapier founder cold emailed people from saas forums and got its first 800 paying users 

7. Plenty of startups like pipedrive($1.5B), salesloft($2.3B), harvey ($8B) and more… got it’s funding through cold emailing. 

so yeah keep doing outreach having a personal brand is ok on the long run 

cold outreach is only thing that can make you quick money (if you know how to sell) 

Noone becomes a big shot on day 1 all you can do is try 

And to try do every possible thing in your reach.

1. The subject line creates an open loop:

“Inbox management at…” feels unfinished, so your brain wants to click and close the loop. Curiosity beats clickbait.

Starts with a super nieche and relevant pain point we would have as we run massive cold email campaigns at AcquisitionX  

3. The CTA:

“Want me to send an early invite?”

A low-commitment question is much easier to answer than “Book a demo.”

The email super short as you see I’m reading the email on my phone and it looks scannable.

I see 100s of startups die each day. 

I run GTM for B2B SaaS companies. 

My whole job is getting their product in front of buyers and turning that into pipeline.

Which means I sit in a strange seat. I see which SaaS companies get replies, book meetings, and close, and which ones send ten thousand emails into a void. 

Long before the funding announcements and the shutdown posts, the answer shows up in the outbound data.

For seventy years, software companies sold one thing at the core.

Now look at what actually happens to AI SaaS ideas. 

Stage one, everyone enters. 

Building takes a weekend. 

That is exactly why it is worthless as a moat. 

Anything you can build in a weekend, ten thousand other people built the same weekend.

When AI floods every channel, human behavior shifts in a very predictable way. 

Volume of content explodes. 

Trust in the average message collapses. 

And the premium on verified human signal goes vertical.

The entire SaaS industry is priced per seat. 

Per human. 

And AI’s core economic function is reducing the number of humans a workflow needs. 

Play that forward and per-seat software has a structural leak that no pricing tier can patch.

Read more: https://t.co/o1iFOFWwop

[TWEET] Fri Jul 17 13:01:12 +0000 2026 | id 2078102722403045807 | likes 12 | reposts 2 | replies 3
Link: https://x.com/Suprva02/status/2078102722403045807

The hottest tech startups like Claude, Perplexity, and ElevenLabs, are opening cafés.

We are about to see a massive shift in tech marketing.

A few years ago, every tech company wanted one thing:

- More screen time.
- More app opens.
- More clicks.

Now the smartest ones are doing the opposite.

They are taking people offline.

☕ Perplexity opened a café in Seoul

• Pro users get 50% off
• Everyone else gets a free month through a QR code
• The product becomes part of a daily habit

📚 Anthropic opened Zero Slop Zone in New York

• No screens
• Free coffee
• Free books
• A hat that simply says “Thinking”

🤖 ElevenLabs opened a pop-up in Soho

• Robots make the coffee
• An AI voice agent runs the store
• Customers negotiate the price of a T-shirt with it

At first, this looks random.

It is not.

They are copying the fashion playbook.

Fashion brands do not only sell clothes.

They sell:

→ Identity
→ Status
→ Community
→ A world people want to enter

Tech companies are starting to do the same.

Claude is not only selling an AI assistant.

It is selling the identity of someone who thinks deeply.

Perplexity is not only selling search.

It is trying to become part of the daily routine of curious people.

ElevenLabs is not only selling voice software.

It is turning the product into an experience people remember.

That is the shift.

The next tech war will not only happen through:

• Better features
• Lower prices
• Bigger launches

It will happen through:

• Cafés
• Pop-ups
• Events
• Merch
• Physical communities

The biggest tech brands will no longer live only on your screen.

They will build places people want to visit.

And once this starts working, every major startup will copy it.

[TWEET] Thu Jul 16 12:46:37 +0000 2026 | id 2077736664630444111 | likes 6 | reposts 0 | replies 3
Link: https://x.com/Suprva02/status/2077736664630444111

We reverse-engineered our best clients and turned it into 37+ positive replies👇🏻

Here’s what we did:

Most lead lists are built using basic filters:

- industry
- headcount
- location
- job title

But companies can match all four and still be completely wrong.

So we worked backwards from clients who had already paid us.

Find the common pattern

We checked:

→ what they sell
→ who they sell to
→ their business model
→ why they hired us

Find similar companies

We pasted our best client domains into 
Prospeo’s company lookalike.

It helped us find companies that were similar based on what they actually do, not just industry tags.

Find one clear trigger

For each company, we checked:

→ long-term job openings
→ founder posts
→ new hires
→ company news

We only contacted companies where we found a clear reason to reach out.

Turn it into handwritten outreach 

Claude connected the trigger with our offer and wrote a short note.

Then we turned it into a realistic handwritten letter and sent the image through LinkedIn.

Every note mentioned:

- their company
- their current problem
- our offer

No generic pitch.

This works the best if you have a nieche market to sell to and they are active on LinkedIn. 

Drop ur questions below 👇🏻 

Share this workflow with your team

(If you want us to implement the same workflow to your system feel free to dm)

We are sending these handwritten notes as outreach it got us 22+ positive replies👇🏻

Here's what we did differently:

Usually a normal text doesn’t get attention these days unless you have a good offer

Now with voice notes a lot have started sending as well so now people are annoyed. 

That leaves us to go back to old school ways…

This is our usual process:

1. https://t.co/yUthsqGNrx to find company lookalikes:

We pasted our best client URLs into ocean.

It gives us 100 companies with the exact same DNA

Add them to ur LinkedIn 

2. Claude Code to found the hidden trigger

For each company we ask Claude:

We find some psychology concepts that is publicly available posted by the team or founder

Then we map companies having a job opening for more than 3 months 

3. Asked claude to turn into a realistic handwritten letter 

Every prospect got a DM that looked like this : 

A photo of a handwritten note.

[TWEET] Fri Jun 19 07:57:36 +0000 2026 | id 2067879458280010154 | likes 7 | reposts 1 | replies 0
Link: https://x.com/Suprva02/status/2067879458280010154

This is going to be the most important post in GTM you'll read this week. Listen closely, GTM is changing massively.

I spoke to GTM teams at Canva, Perplexity, Gamma AI and 7 more companies.

And what they mentioned is going to change the future of GTM.

We all know how fast moving this space is…

Here are some things that are going to make a big difference in the coming months:

1. Teams are shifting more offline, so event networking communities are going to see a massive boost.

2. AI will read your emails before passing them to a human, so whatever email you write needs to pass the AI filters.

3. Cold emailing is going to see a drastic change. A lot of new AI tools for cold emails will be introduced that are going to change how we send emails.

4. Offline gifts are going to become a way to do outreach.

5. The creativity and art side is going to grow more, and more campaigns will have a creative shift in them.

6. Signals are going to change, micro-intent is going to take place. Example: earlier someone raised funding and you reached out; now it will be someone raised funding, but you reach out based on their behavior, like who they're hiring, what the age gap is.

7. More and more teams are going to hire in-house UGC creators as their content team to produce content for them.

8. Teams are going to shift from a 10+ tool stack to all-in-one tools. We will see a major shift here.

9. Having an educational content YouTube channel is going to have a massive impact on how we get customers. Most of the attention is shifting back to long-form because of AI-generated junk on text-based platforms.

10. Offline newsletters are going to take place. You'll notice brands opening offline newsletters (it has already started in the B2C space) — curated letters in mailboxes for every new launch, instead of a newsletter in the inbox.

11. We will see a massive boost in community-building plans, especially things like run clubs, tech cafés, and offline meetups dedicated to each brand.

As someone who does GTM for a living, I'm excited to see these changes take place.

If you're someone who can help me implement the above GTM methods for our clients, feel free to reach out to me :) I'm always looking for you.

I've also asked my team to do more research and package the interview into a playbook.

If you want the step-by-step process, just comment "GTM" and I'll send it your way.

So back to the point of this post.

In theory it’s easy to say “build better lead generation systems.”

But what does that actually mean in practice?

Naval-style thinking would break it down into fundamentals of attention and conversion.

Systems.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[REPLY] Wed May 13 10:35:51 +0000 2026 | id 2054510930973933620 | likes 1 | reposts 0 | replies 1
Link: https://x.com/Suprva02/status/2054510930973933620

(Reply to @Suprva02)

Alex Hormozi calls it “offer first thinking.”

Marc Andreessen calls it “distribution before product fit.”

Naval calls it “leverage”.

But all three point to the same thing:

growth is not a sequence.

It’s a system where attention compounds into demand before any conversation

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[REPLY] Wed May 13 10:35:50 +0000 2026 | id 2054510925894549724 | likes 1 | reposts 0 | replies 1
Link: https://x.com/Suprva02/status/2054510925894549724

(Reply to @Suprva02)

Naval research on distribution shows a pattern:

High-leverage systems don’t start with outreach.

They start with repeated exposure before contact.

1. Build visibility so attention already exists
2. Create signals that establish credibility early
3. Increase familiarity before any direct reach
4. Let trust compound over repeated impressions

Cold outreach is not the system.

It is just the final trigger.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[REPLY] Wed May 13 10:35:47 +0000 2026 | id 2054510914268021074 | likes 1 | reposts 0 | replies 1

Most people think lead generation is the starting point.

But it’s actually the last step.

By the time someone “responds”…

the decision has already been shaped somewhere else.

(Reply to @Suprva02)

Few tips for a better deliverability rate:

- Emails should be plain text only (no pdf and links)
- A/B test 6 script variants
- The bounce rate should stay under 2%
- DNC list should be constantly updated
- All emails should be double-verified
- Use Spintax to create variance

A simple way to define USP and ISP

USP: Unique Selling Proposition

-&gt; 1 feature
-&gt; 1 benefit
-&gt; 1 key selling point in all marketing efforts 

ISP: Ideal Selling Proposition

-&gt; multiple features
-&gt; multiple benefits 
-&gt; aimed at a wider audience
